+### Pass 3 (review of draft1): owner items and declined parts
+
+The full disposition of every finding is in `paper/v2/review/resolutions_W3.md`.
+
+- **RM-9, OWNER (class C, new; a theory-doc item too).** Definition 1's state
+  omits the weak-terrain mask w, drawn once per episode and read by T_C and by
+  the observation's "weak" channel, and the observation kernel has no action

-The fire is an ambient hazard that no reward, cost or constraint channel reads; the reward reads only task variables and agent deaths, pricing every death the same. -When a training distribution has to cover several stressors, its designer chooses whether the policy meets them together or one at a time. Ecology studies the interactive and cumulative effects of multiple stressors <d-cite key="crain2008stressors,cote2016stressors"></d-cite>, and climate science the risk from compound events <d-cite key="zscheischler2018compound,zscheischler2020typology"></d-cite>. In reinforcement learning the choice is a question of zero-shot generalization <d-cite key="kirk2023zsg"></d-cite>: must a policy see stressors co-occur in training to cope with their co-occurrence at test time? Gao et al. (2024) <d-cite key="gao2024composition"></d-cite> studied it for imitation-learned manipulation; we study it for survival in multi-agent reinforcement learning.

-Where the hazard sits in the learning problem shapes what such a comparison can teach: in some fire-related work it is the task <d-cite key="haksar2018"></d-cite>, and safe reinforcement learning typically places it in a cost or a constraint <d-cite key="garcia2015safe"></d-cite>. In the Compound Hostile Environment (CHE) the fire is an *ambient hazard* (Definition 2 in §3): a fixed stochastic process that optimizes against no one and enters no reward, cost or constraint channel. The reward reads task variables and agent deaths, and prices every death at $d_p = 0.5$<!-- N:ENV-1 -->, whatever its cause. No reward or cost signal mentions the hazard in either arm, so the arms differ only in what their training distributions show the agents. Proposition 1 shows that the fire, which looks like nonstationarity to any one agent, is part of the state: CHE is a well-defined Dec-POMDP whose states reachable within the horizon form a finite set (§3).

-The closest earlier study of our question is Gao et al. (2024) <d-cite key="gao2024composition"></d-cite>. In visual imitation learning for robotic manipulation, they ask whether policies compose environmental factors, such as object type and position, table texture and height, and camera position, to succeed on unseen combinations. They compare data-collection strategies at a matched number of demonstrations, among them varying one factor at a time and cycling through factors so that more combinations appear (their §III-C and §IV-A). Policies do compose, more weakly when both factors are physical (their §V-B), and the strategy that covers more combinations does better. Their design is built for data efficiency and does not separate co-occurrence from how often each factor value appears; ours matches compute and, in the ISO-4 correction, the active share (§1). CompoSuite <d-cite key="mendez2022composuite"></d-cite> studies the composition of tasks, over robots, objects, objectives and obstacles, in multi-task reinforcement learning. The DBCA method <d-cite key="keysers2020"></d-cite> builds splits whose atom distributions are similar and whose compound distributions differ. Our secondary sweep follows that idea, holding each coupling's frequency fixed while co-occurrence varies; our ISO and JOINT endpoints do not, since JOINT sees each coupling three times<!-- N:DES-7 --> as often as ISO (§1, §9.1).

-Robust reinforcement learning asks the same question about perturbations. Erdem & Üre (2025) <d-cite key="erdem2025mixed"></d-cite> train single-agent continuous-control policies against a learned adversary that perturbs observations, actions, or both, and report that agents trained against one modality remain vulnerable to simultaneous attacks on both (their Tables 3–4). Under single-type attacks, agents trained against their balanced mixed adversary do at least as well as those trained against a naive one in all six cells, one a tie (their Table 5). Of their six training arms, one is an unattacked baseline, and in each of the others an attack is computed at every step, with no scheduled clean steps (their Algorithm 1). No arm has a counterpart of the fire-only share our correction removes. No arm trains on a mixture of single-type attacks, and the mixed arms carry the combined budget, which the authors note is more severe. Their design thus bundles per-modality frequency, co-occurrence and a larger budget, which is nearer to what our corrected contrast leaves in, where JOINT still sees each coupling twice<!-- N:DES-14 --> as often as ISO-4; it neither supports nor contradicts our corrected null. Agrawal et al. (2023) <d-cite key="agrawal2023multimodal"></d-cite> train multi-agent policies against random reward, state and action noise, one or two types at a time, under a curriculum that raises the noise level and sets training length, so exposure differs between their models. In supervised vision, Tramèr & Boneh (2019) <d-cite key="tramer2019multiple"></d-cite> and Hsiung et al. (2023) <d-cite key="hsiung2023compositional"></d-cite> study robustness to multiple and to composite perturbations.

-Chen et al. (2022) <d-cite key="chen2022understandingdr"></d-cite> model domain randomization as returning the optimal history-dependent policy of a latent MDP, a set of fully observed MDPs that differ in their transitions. Every upper bound they prove on its shortfall assumes each MDP is communicating, with bounded diameter. Theorem 1 (§6) is kin to the construction of their Proposition 1, in which an irreversible choice is made before anything distinguishes the candidate MDPs. Read as a latent MDP with the signals encoded as states, Theorem 1's two-corridor toy has an absorbing failure, so their upper bounds do not apply. It also measures a different gap, the adaptive optimum against the policy optimal for a fixed map. Chen et al. therefore do not subsume Theorem 1, and Theorem 1 does not extend their bounds. Pynadath & Tambe (2002) <d-cite key="pynadath2002"></d-cite> relate the complexity of team decision problems with communication to observability. Free communication lowers the complexity when the world is collectively observable (their Theorem 4 and Table 2), and makes no difference when it is individually observable or not observable at all (their Theorems 5–6). Remark 2′ (§7) refines this for collective partial observability: communication's marginal value vanishes when interchangeable agents' views are redundant, and returns when roles are irreplaceable or deaths are costly. A survey of adaptability in multi-agent reinforcement learning (Hu et al., 2025, revised 2026) <d-cite key="hu2025adaptablemarl"></d-cite> calls an environment family highly diagnostic if it "systematically varies" factors such as population, observability or communication structure "while holding other factors fixed". It mentions compositional generalization but does not analyse shifts in several factors at once.

-A comparison of joint and isolated training that leaves the active share free can report an active-share effect as a composition effect. This holds by construction, as JOINT's threefold per-coupling frequency over ISO<!-- N:DES-7 --> does. We make one recommendation, motivated by this result alone: that studies of compositional generalization match the share of training that contains an active stressor before attributing a gain to composition. It was motivated in one environment at one scale, and whether it holds elsewhere is untested. Matching the active share here left JOINT's other differences in place, and the corrected contrast excludes only a large composition effect (§9.2).

-**Remark 2′.** Stated in §7.2, after Pynadath and Tambe<d-cite key="pynadath2002"></d-cite>. One step is not spelled out in §7. Let $\tilde q$ be the probability of being informed under the best idling schedule. The commitment draws have different optical depths, so as $\kappa_B \to \infty$ the lowest-depth draw dominates both products and $\tilde q / q \to 1$. The correction peaks at moderate $\kappa_B$, and its relative bite on the value of communication is largest at low $\kappa_B$, where $q$ is near 1. In the two-agent toy, which is not CHE (4096 episodes and 8192 Monte Carlo draws per point<!-- N:DX-15 -->), $\tilde q / q$ is 1.126 at $\kappa_B = 1.0$ and peaks at 1.235 near $\kappa_B = 2$<!-- N:DX-15 -->. The correction removes 79 %, 54 % and 7 % of $\mathrm{VoC}_{\mathrm{gated}}$ at $\kappa_B$ = 0.5, 1.0 and 3.0<!-- N:DX-15 -->. The handshake gate on the pinned arm passes (largest absolute z 0.44; χ² 0.51 on 8 degrees of freedom<!-- N:DX-15 -->), and the free arm scores 1.0000 at every $\kappa_B$<!-- N:DX-15 -->.

-Ablations are exact by construction: every stochastic branch consumes its random numbers whether or not its stressor is on, so switching a stressor off reproduces the nested model's trajectories bit for bit under the same keys (§3.4, Listing 3). Four repeated evaluations of each of two checkpoints are bit-identical on one GPU and identical to the grid's own evaluations<!-- N:HW-2 -->. Across GPUs, 280 of 288<!-- N:HW-3 --> paired evaluations are identical, and the largest absolute difference is 2.44 × 10⁻⁴<!-- N:HW-3 -->. GPU training is not run-to-run reproducible (§8), which is why floors are measured (Appendix C).
